for ocean dynamics spanned from","1775 to 1920. It was in 1775 that Pierre Simon-Laplace first developed","simplified tidal equations. In historical terms this was considered the","first \"\\emph{dynamic theory of tides}\" in which differential equations","\\emph{\"describes the ocean\\textquotesingle s real reaction to tidal","forces}\".~~ The golden age perhaps culminated with A.T.","Doodson\\textquotesingle s work in cataloguing 388 tidal frequencies −","and which eventually formed the basis for establishing a consensus in","understanding and predicting ocean tides.","","In parallel to this work, pioneering research was carried out on the","non-linear differential equations known as the Mathieu {[}1{]} and","Hill's equations {[}2{]}. The initial application of the Mathieu","equation was for determining the harmonics of a vibrating drum head","{[}1{]}. The Hills equation was more general and found application to","perturbations to periodic orbits {[}3{]}. Lord Rayleigh independently","investigated Faraday's earlier work {[}4{]} in terms of what later","became known as the Mathieu equation to understand the phenomena of","period doubling {[}5{]}.","","\\begin{quote}","\\emph{\\textbf{``}Faraday arrived experimentally at the conclusion that","there were two complete vibrations\\\\","of the support for each complete vibration of the liquid.'' -- Lord","Rayleigh}","\\end{quote}","","Eventually, the Mathieu equation found its way to modeling the dynamics","of ocean basins {[}6{]}, but in retrospect that work appeared flawed and","was much too specific in its scope given its peculiar premise in trying","to cast the ocean as an elliptical drumhead. Only later in 1954 did the","fundamental application of the Mathieu equation to liquid sloshing","become evident {[}7{]}.","","The more random waves commonly encountered as the familiar chop and surf","are largely due to forcing of the surface wind. We will first describe","two deterministic wave behaviors (conventional ocean tides and ENSO),","and then cover the stochastic behaviors of the ubiquitous wind waves.","","\\hypertarget{deterministic-tidal-forcing}{%","\\subsubsection*{Deterministic Tidal","Forcing}\\label{deterministic-tidal-forcing}}","\\addcontentsline{toc}{subsubsection}{Deterministic Tidal Forcing}","","The well-known ocean tides are a deterministic response to lunar and","solar gravitational forcing applied to the ocean's volume. The analysis","is quite straightforward as the full solution to Laplace's tidal","equation is not typically necessary or required. Instead, a forced","response perturbation is applied to a specific geographic location, and","a harmonic reconstruction based on the known diurnal and semidiurnal","tidal sinusoidal periods (Table 12-1) is optimally fit to a measured","sea-level height (SLH) profile {[}8{]}.","","From data in {[}9{]} a tidal model was fitted to a raw buoy","sea-level-height (SLH) applying the simplest harmonic analysis with 4","major constituent tidal parameters and another 4 minor parameters, see","\\textbf{FIGURE\\_12-1 on page 317}","","The yellow highlighted region is training which reached almost a 0.99","correlation coefficient, with the validation region to the right","reaching 0.92. The complex Fourier spectrum is shown in","\\textbf{FIGURE\\_12-2 on page 317.}","","The set of constituent coefficients we use derives from Table 12-1 with","the 5 principal tidal constituents, and only \\emph{N\\textsubscript{2}}","is a minor factor in this case study.","","\\begin{longtable}[]{@{}","  >{\\raggedright\\arraybackslash}p{(\\columnwidth - 2\\tabcolsep) * \\real{0.5732}}","  >{\\raggedright\\arraybackslash}p{(\\columnwidth - 2\\tabcolsep) * \\real{0.4268}}@{}}","\\toprule\\noalign{}","\\begin{minipage}[b]{\\linewidth}\\raggedright","Table 12‑1","\\end{minipage} & \\begin{minipage}[b]{\\linewidth}\\raggedright","\\end{minipage} \\\\","\\midrule\\noalign{}","\\endhead","\\bottomrule\\noalign{}","\\endlastfoot","\\begin{minipage}[t]{\\linewidth}\\raggedright","\\begin{longtable}[]{@{}","  >{\\raggedright\\arraybackslash}p{(\\columnwidth - 4\\tabcolsep) * \\real{0.5746}}","  >{\\raggedright\\arraybackslash}p{(\\columnwidth - 4\\tabcolsep) * \\real{0.1754}}","  >{\\raggedright\\arraybackslash}p{(\\columnwidth - 4\\tabcolsep) * \\real{0.2500}}@{}}","\\toprule\\noalign{}","\\begin{minipage}[b]{\\linewidth}\\raggedright","Semi-diurnal Species","\\end{minipage} & \\begin{minipage}[b]{\\linewidth}\\raggedright","Darwin Symbol","\\end{minipage} & \\begin{minipage}[b]{\\linewidth}\\raggedright","Period (hr)","\\end{minipage} \\\\","\\midrule\\noalign{}","\\endhead","\\bottomrule\\noalign{}","\\endlastfoot","Principal lunar semidiurnal & M\\textsubscript{2} & 12.4206012 \\\\","Principal solar semidiurnal & S\\textsubscript{2} & 12 \\\\","Larger lunar elliptic semidiurnal & N\\textsubscript{2} & 12.65834751 \\\\","\\end{longtable}","\\end{minipage} & \\begin{minipage}[t]{\\linewidth}\\raggedright","\\begin{longtable}[]{@{}","  >{\\raggedright\\arraybackslash}p{(\\columnwidth - 4\\tabcolsep) * \\real{0.4250}}","  >{\\raggedright\\arraybackslash}p{(\\columnwidth - 4\\tabcolsep) * \\real{0.2216}}","  >{\\raggedright\\arraybackslash}p{(\\columnwidth - 4\\tabcolsep) * \\real{0.3534}}@{}}","\\toprule\\noalign{}","\\begin{minipage}[b]{\\linewidth}\\raggedright","Diurnal Species","\\end{minipage} & \\begin{minipage}[b]{\\linewidth}\\raggedright","Darwin Symbol","\\end{minipage} & \\begin{minipage}[b]{\\linewidth}\\raggedright","Period (hr)","\\end{minipage} \\\\","\\midrule\\noalign{}","\\endhead","\\bottomrule\\noalign{}","\\endlastfoot","Lunar diurnal & K\\textsubscript{1} & 23.93447213 \\\\","Lunar diurnal & O\\textsubscript{1} & 25.81933871 \\\\","\\end{longtable}","\\end{minipage} \\\\","\\end{longtable}","","In practice, solver routines, multiple linear regression, and Fourier","series harmonic construction can be applied.","","Next is ENSO which requires a similar kind of analysis.","","\\hypertarget{deterministic-enso}{%","\\subsubsection*{Deterministic ENSO}\\label{deterministic-enso}}","\\addcontentsline{toc}{subsubsection}{Deterministic ENSO}","","Apart from the well-known seasonal climate pattern, the climate","phenomenon known as the El Nino/Southern Oscillation (ENSO) is one of","the most important contributors to climate variability {[}10{]}. Due to","its behavior, an enormous amount of energy is periodically released from","the equatorial Pacific Ocean into the atmosphere. This energy, in the","form of warm air and moisture, migrates across the world, assisted by","prevailing winds. As a result of this natural process, extremes of","climate, such as extended heat spells, droughts, and flooding can wreak","havoc in various regions of the globe\\footnote{In retrospect, the impact","  of ENSO has been known since Charles Darwin in 1896 wrote in his","  journal \\emph{\\textbf{``}. . . variations in climate sometimes appear","  to be the effect of the operation of some very general cause\\textbf{''","  }}when performing his seminal Galapagos Islands research {[}11{]}.}.","","While one cycle of the ENSO behavior, dubbed El Nino {[}12{]}, can cause","extreme heat, the opposite sign of the cycle, named La Nina, can lead to","colder than average conditions as a cooler ocean can absorb the","atmosphere's heat. And this pattern is typically reversed depending on","one's geographic location. That is, a North America El Nino pattern may","be equivalent to an Australian La Nina pattern. And like many cyclic","behaviors, it remains zero-sum over the long-term, as the ocean","alternately releases and absorbs heat energy.","","Understanding the ENSO phenomenon has enormous benefits for economic","planning, as local officials have time to prepare for any coming change","in climate, either in preparing for higher temperatures, implementing","flooding safeguards, fish harvests, or stockpiling water for irrigation.","This is no different, except in scale, in having knowledge of local","tidal patterns so that extra precaution can be taken with watercraft in","ports and harbors.","","However, to be able to accurately predict an El Nino or La Nina cycle is","considered a very difficult problem, with the lead times for accurate","predictions only a few months in advance, and that is still considered a","probabilistic estimate since most models do not agree with each other.","As with weather prediction, the best one can do is project a spread of","values. The difficulty stems from the current understanding that ENSO is","sensitive to initial conditions, and that over the long-term, the","behavior is as difficult to predict as any chaotic or random phenomena","{[}13{]}.","","Yet, if ENSO \\emph{was} found to be as predictable as the deterministic","ocean tides, the benefits to our geophysics understanding with regard to","climate science would also be enormous. As it is thought that most","hurricane and cyclone activity is spawned off the warm equatorial","waters, being able to understand the fundamental processes behind","cyclonic formation would be a significant advance.","","So the current understanding of ENSO is divided between some who believe","it has some predictability {[}14{]}, {[}15{]} to those that think it is","at best a statistical {[}16{]} or chaotic {[}13{]} phenomenon.","","Based on the fact that ENSO is a singular behavior (i.e. it is a single","collective action) , we can analyze it as a deterministic phenomenon and","gauge how well that works. As with tides and QBO (see Chapter 11), we","think its predictability meshes with the lunar and solar orbit","predictability, which has been hypothesized elsewhere","{[}17{]}--{[}21{]}. We will use mathematical physics and then a","computational solution to demonstrate this.","","\\hypertarget{behavioral-mechanism}{%","\\paragraph*{Behavioral Mechanism}\\label{behavioral-mechanism}}","\\addcontentsline{toc}{paragraph}{Behavioral Mechanism}","","The basic premise is that ENSO is a long-period sloshing of the Pacific","ocean\\textquotesingle s thermocline which is forced by slight changes to","the earth\\textquotesingle s rotation rate. This is analogous to the","tidal forcing described in the previous section, but is of a much longer","period, as the inertial response of the ocean is much slower than the","tidal response. ~The essential idea is that the change in the","earth\\textquotesingle s rotation results in the situation~whereby the","ocean will want to keep moving due to Newton\\textquotesingle s first","law. This change is significant ---~about 1 millisecond over the span of","a few weeks ---~and has an even greater impact in the reduced gravity","environment of a thermocline {[}22{]}.","","Along with that, any gravitational forcing variation via the lunisolar","(lunar plus solar) cycles contributes to both the effective gravity","reduction at the ocean's thermocline and also in the earth's rotation","rate. This in fact makes it difficult to attribute the cause to either","angular momentum variations or to the lunisolar forcing. The implication","is that the gravitational forcing that causes slight variations in the","earth\\textquotesingle s rotation speed will transitively cause the","sloshing in the Pacific ocean\\textquotesingle s thermocline, leading to","the cyclic ENSO behavior. We choose the latter as it is well","characterized and may actually subsume the angular variations.","","Next note that at the ENSO scale, which is much longer that the daily","and monthly cycles of the lunar month, we can infer that the yearly","cycle also has a role to play. And in addition to the annual cycle, the","semi-annual cycle and the biennial cycle {[}23{]} could also contribute;","in regards to the latter, it is well known that period doubling is","observed in sloshing experiments {[}24{]}, {[}25{]} and that period","doubling of the annual cycle is observed in many GCMs {[}26{]},","{[}27{]}. The analogy to period doubling in mechanical systems is","described by the following excerpt, where the high-lighted phrase shows","the role of the first \\emph{sub-harmonic} frequency of the main (i.e.","\\emph{fundamental}) forcing frequency:","","\\begin{quote}","\\emph{``Faraday waves are often analysed in analogy with the parametric","excitation of a pendulum (Fauve 1998). Although this simple model","presents an obvious pedagogical interest, in particular to introduce the","Mathieu equation and to show that the first resonance corresponds to","half of the forcing frequency, use of the parametric pendulum analogy is","often misleading. Indeed, a major difference is that the eigenfrequency","of a freely-oscillating pendulum is unique, whereas free unforced, water","waves exhibit a continuous spectrum of mode frequencies. Therefore, for","water waves, there always exists an angular eigenfrequency ω(k)","\\textbf{corresponding exactly to half of the forcing angular frequency","Ω}''} {[}4{]}","\\end{quote}","","Thus, the premise for the ENSO process is a wide-scale wave-like","sloshing of the equatorial Pacific waters along the east-west direction.","This defines a dipole or see-saw in vertical characteristics at opposite","sides of the ocean. Because of the moon\\textquotesingle s cyclic","gravitational pull, the earth\\textquotesingle s rotation speed varies","slightly and with the identical period of the moon. This leads to an","alternate lifting of the thermocline layer on the western side of the","Pacific as the earth\\textquotesingle s rotation slows slightly and then","the reverse as the rotation speeds up. This is straightforward Newtonian","physics that must occur -\\/- the only question is how strong this effect","is, and thus how well does it explain the variability observed in","temperatures?\\footnote{A strictly biennial cycle is measured in","  distributed GPS station data which is sensitive to earth deformation","  {[}28{]}} If we assume that the effect is there, the least we can do","is to determine if the nominally known lunisolar cycles match those of","ENSO. As we described Lindzen's assertion concerning patterns regarding","QBO in the last chapter: \\emph{``For oscillations of tidal periods, the","nature of the forcing is clear''} {[}29{]}.","","~","","The explanation for a global heating mechanism is straightforward. A","side-effect of the vertical motion of the thermocline is an oscillatory","release of ocean heat to the atmosphere or a sinking of atmospheric or","latent heat to the ocean. If the thermocline variation is severe, this","translates to significant variations of the Earth\\textquotesingle s","average temperature that parallel the thermocline motion.","","~","","That\\textquotesingle s an intuitive explanation for the basic mechanism","behind ENSO. There is also a simplifying mathematical derivation related","to the equations of fluid dynamics that we can show in detail next. The","simplifications largely derive from the symmetry-reducing constraints","found along the equator.","","\\hypertarget{characterization}{%","\\paragraph*{Characterization}\\label{characterization}}","\\addcontentsline{toc}{paragraph}{Characterization}","","As with ocean tidal analysis, pattern matching is important to verify","this as the primary mechanism behind ENSO. The key to matching patterns","is to use every geophysical constraint available; fortunately, these are","precisely known from sources such as NASA JPL{[}30{]} and","France\\textquotesingle s IERS{[}31{]} (for lunar ephemeris and earth","rotation properties, respectively).","","~","","The primary tidal cycles applied to ENSO correspond to the draconic (aka","nodal) month and the anomalistic month. The former varies slightly from","a fixed period while the period of the latter undergoes a distinctive","acceleration and deceleration cycle that needs to be matched rather","precisely.","","~","","The secondary impact due to the synodic (aka tropical or sidereal) month","isn\\textquotesingle t used directly but modifies the primary cycles, of","which the acceleration and deceleration of the anomalistic cycle is an","example. It also modifies the solar cycle, as the phases of the moon","cyclically reinforce the sun's gravitational pull {[}2{]}.","","~","","Once this set of constraints is fixed, we can incorporate an estimate of","the relative magnitude of each of the factors. The most direct estimate","is to apply the measured change in the earth\\textquotesingle s","rotational speed due to the lunar factors. That proves an effective","method to capture approximate values for (among other factors) the","contribution of the monthly draconic value in comparison to the","fortnightly value. Also solar eclipse timing provides a valuable","calibrating constraint, especially in terms of the phase relationship","between the anomalistic and draconic cycles.","","~","","\\hypertarget{premise-for-enso-model}{%","\\paragraph*{Premise for ENSO Model}\\label{premise-for-enso-model}}","\\addcontentsline{toc}{paragraph}{Premise for ENSO Model}","","The key step in relating the lunar cycles to the seasonal cycle is","establishing the phase locking impulse that triggers ENSO","behavior\\footnote{Transients and impulses are common in stratified","  layers, the most notable being the overturning of a","  lake\\textquotesingle s thermocline that can happen once or twice a","  year and at specific times within the seasonal cycle. There is also","  phytoplankton evidence for this in the Pacific ocean {[}32{]}.}. This","alternates each year in sign to accentuate the change from El Nino","episodes to La Nino episodes and vice versa. A biennial cycle appears in","comprehensive GCMs and simpler delay differential equations used to","model ENSO. More generally, period doubling modulation appears in","sloshing simulations of non-linear equations with a fundamental cycle","forcing applied. Where it is often seen is in the Mathieu equations used","in engineering hydrology to study sloshing of liquids aboard container","ships {[}24{]}, {[}25{]}, {[}33{]}, {[}34{]} and with atmospheric/ocean","models {[}35{]}.","","~","","Thus the premise for the ENSO model is that a similar yearly impulse is","reinforcing the tidal impulse at a predictable time of the year. The","hypothesis is that this predictable forcing leads to the erratic cycles","of ENSO. The erratic nature of the cycles and any difficulty in matching","these is directly the result of the physically aliasing that occurs from","applying a low frequency sampling (the yearly impulse) to a higher","frequency phenomenon (the monthly and fortnightly lunar cycles) which","are incommensurate with the annual cycle. This can lead to a repeat","cycle that has been estimated anywhere from 353 years to 1800 years","{[}19{]}--{[}21{]}.","","~","","Each of the two primary lunar factors is a combination of the lunar","monthly and fortnightly terms, so that we have only four degrees of","freedom (DOF) in terms of amplitude and only two in phase (since the","monthly and fortnightly cycles share the same phase for each lunar","mode). The solar tidal factor provides the other degrees of freedom,","along with the tropical lunar term, which reinforces the sun's pull","depending on the moon's phase. That is limited complexity but enough to","create a lengthy repeat cycle, as is well known from conventional tidal","analysis.","","\\hypertarget{historical-basis-of-calculation}{%","\\paragraph*{Historical Basis of","Calculation}\\label{historical-basis-of-calculation}}","\\addcontentsline{toc}{paragraph}{Historical Basis of Calculation}","","Over 200 years ago, Laplace developed his namesake tidal equations to","mathematically explain the behavior of tides by applying straightforward","Newtonian physics.\\footnote{Laplace had suggested that the nature of the","  universe is completely deterministic, and he was quoted as saying that","  \\emph{\"All the effects of nature are only the mathematical","  consequences of a small number of immutable laws\"}, thus perhaps","  providing his motivation for working these calculations.} In their","expanded form -\\/- known as the \\emph{primitive equations} of the more","general \\emph{Navier-Stokes} formulation -\\/- Laplace\\textquotesingle s","starting formulation is used as the basis of almost all detailed climate","models. Since that\\textquotesingle s what they are designed to do, we","will next provide the details for solving","\\emph{Laplace\\textquotesingle s tidal equations} in the context of the","El Nino Southern Oscillation of the equatorial Pacific ocean.","","The concise derivation for a model of ENSO depends on reducing","Laplace\\textquotesingle s tidal equations along the equator. This","approach is not commonly referred to in the research literature, even","though it appears to be straightforward: (1) solve","Laplace\\textquotesingle s tidal equations in a simplified context, then","(2) apply the known tidal forcing along with an annual harmonic (and","subharmonic) forcing and observe if the result correlates or matches the","ENSO time series. Below is a two part solution.","","\\hypertarget{part-1-deriving-a-closed-form-solution-to-laplaces-tidal-equations}{%","\\paragraph*{Part 1 : Deriving a closed-form solution to Laplace's Tidal","Equations}\\label{part-1-deriving-a-closed-form-solution-to-laplaces-tidal-equations}}","\\addcontentsline{toc}{paragraph}{Part 1 : Deriving a closed-form","solution to Laplace's Tidal Equations}","","For a fluid sheet of average thickness \\emph{D}, the vertical tidal","elevation \\emph{ζ}~, as well as the horizontal velocity components","\\emph{u}~ and \\emph{v}~ (in the latitude \\emph{φ}~and longitude","\\emph{λ}~directions), the following is the set of","Laplace\\textquotesingle s tidal equations. The idea is that along the","equator, i.e. for \\emph{φ}~at zero, we can reduce these to something","much simpler.","","\\begin{longtable}[]{@{}","  >{\\raggedright\\arraybackslash}p{(\\columnwidth - 2\\tabcolsep) * \\real{0.8947}}","  >{\\raggedright\\arraybackslash}p{(\\columnwidth - 2\\tabcolsep) * \\real{0.1053}}@{}}","\\toprule\\noalign{}","\\begin{minipage}[b]{\\linewidth}\\raggedright","\\[\\begin{array}{r}","\\frac{\\partial\\zeta}{\\partial t} + \\frac{1}{a\\cos(\\varphi)}\\lbrack\\frac{\\partial}{\\partial\\lambda}(uD) + \\frac{\\partial}{\\partial\\varphi}(vD\\cos(\\varphi))\\rbrack = 0, \\\\","\\frac{\\partial u}{\\partial t} - v(2\\Omega sin(\\varphi)) + \\frac{1}{a\\cos(\\varphi)}\\frac{\\partial}{\\partial\\lambda}(g\\zeta + U) = 0, \\\\","\\frac{\\partial v}{\\partial t} + u(2\\Omega sin(\\varphi)) + \\frac{1}{a}\\frac{\\partial}{\\partial\\varphi}(g\\zeta + U) = 0,","\\end{array}\\]","\\end{minipage} & \\begin{minipage}[b]{\\linewidth}\\raggedright","12-1","\\end{minipage} \\\\","\\midrule\\noalign{}","\\endhead","\\bottomrule\\noalign{}","\\endlastfoot","\\end{longtable}","","where Ω~ is the angular frequency of the planet\\textquotesingle s","rotation, \\emph{g}~ is the planet\\textquotesingle s gravitational","acceleration at the mean ocean surface, \\emph{a}~ is the planetary","radius, and \\emph{U}~ is the external gravitational tidal-forcing","potential.","","The main candidates for removal due to the small-angle approximation","along the equator are the second terms in the second and third equation.","The plan is to then substitute the isolated \\emph{u}~and \\emph{v}~terms","into the first equation, after taking another derivative of that","equation with respect to \\emph{t}~.","","\\begin{longtable}[]{@{}","  >{\\raggedright\\arraybackslash}p{(\\columnwidth - 2\\tabcolsep) * \\real{0.8947}}","  >{\\raggedright\\arraybackslash}p{(\\columnwidth - 2\\tabcolsep) * \\real{0.1053}}@{}}","\\toprule\\noalign{}","\\begin{minipage}[b]{\\linewidth}\\raggedright","\\[\\begin{array}{r}","\\frac{\\partial\\zeta}{\\partial t} + \\frac{1}{a}\\lbrack\\frac{\\partial}{\\partial\\lambda}(uD) + \\frac{\\partial}{\\partial\\varphi}(vD)\\rbrack = 0, \\\\","\\frac{\\partial u}{\\partial t} + \\frac{1}{a}\\frac{\\partial}{\\partial\\lambda}(g\\zeta + U) = 0, \\\\","\\frac{\\partial v}{\\partial t} + \\frac{1}{a}\\frac{\\partial}{\\partial\\varphi}(g\\zeta + U) = 0,","\\end{array}\\]","\\end{minipage} & \\begin{minipage}[b]{\\linewidth}\\raggedright","12-2","\\end{minipage} \\\\","\\midrule\\noalign{}","\\endhead","\\bottomrule\\noalign{}","\\endlastfoot","\\end{longtable}","","Taking another derivative of the first equation:","","\\begin{longtable}[]{@{}","  >{\\raggedright\\arraybackslash}p{(\\columnwidth - 2\\tabcolsep) * \\real{0.8947}}","  >{\\raggedright\\arraybackslash}p{(\\columnwidth - 2\\tabcolsep) * \\real{0.1053}}@{}}","\\toprule\\noalign{}","\\begin{minipage}[b]{\\linewidth}\\raggedright","\\[\\begin{matrix}","a\\frac{\\partial^{2}\\zeta}{\\partial t^{2}} & + \\frac{\\partial}{\\partial t}\\lbrack\\frac{\\partial}{\\partial\\lambda}(uD) + \\frac{\\partial}{\\partial\\varphi}(vD)\\rbrack = 0,","\\end{matrix}\\]","\\end{minipage} & \\begin{minipage}[b]{\\linewidth}\\raggedright","12-3","\\end{minipage} \\\\","\\midrule\\noalign{}","\\endhead","\\bottomrule\\noalign{}","\\endlastfoot","\\end{longtable}","","Next, on the bracketed pair we invert the order of derivatives (and pull","out the constant \\emph{D}~D )","","\\begin{longtable}[]{@{}","  >{\\raggedright\\arraybackslash}p{(\\columnwidth - 2\\tabcolsep) * \\real{0.8947}}","  >{\\raggedright\\arraybackslash}p{(\\columnwidth - 2\\tabcolsep) * \\real{0.1053}}@{}}","\\toprule\\noalign{}","\\begin{minipage}[b]{\\linewidth}\\raggedright","\\[\\begin{array}{r}","a\\frac{\\partial^{2}\\zeta}{\\partial t^{2}} + D\\lbrack\\frac{\\partial}{\\partial\\lambda}(\\frac{\\partial u}{\\partial t}) + \\frac{\\partial}{\\partial\\varphi}(\\frac{\\partial v}{\\partial t})\\rbrack = 0,","\\end{array}\\]","\\end{minipage} & \\begin{minipage}[b]{\\linewidth}\\raggedright","12-4","\\end{minipage} \\\\","\\midrule\\noalign{}","\\endhead","\\bottomrule\\noalign{}","\\endlastfoot","\\end{longtable}","","Notice now that the bracketed terms can be replaced by the 2nd and 3rd","of Laplace\\textquotesingle s equations","","\\begin{longtable}[]{@{}","  >{\\raggedright\\arraybackslash}p{(\\columnwidth - 2\\tabcolsep) * \\real{0.8947}}","  >{\\raggedright\\arraybackslash}p{(\\columnwidth - 2\\tabcolsep) * \\real{0.1053}}@{}}","\\toprule\\noalign{}","\\begin{minipage}[b]{\\linewidth}\\raggedright","\\[\\begin{array}{r}","\\frac{\\partial u}{\\partial t} = - \\frac{1}{a}\\frac{\\partial}{\\partial\\lambda}(g\\zeta + U) \\\\","\\ \\ \\ \\frac{\\partial v}{\\partial t} = - \\frac{1}{a}\\frac{\\partial}{\\partial\\varphi}(g\\zeta + U)","\\end{array}\\ \\]","\\end{minipage} & \\begin{minipage}[b]{\\linewidth}\\raggedright","12-5","\\end{minipage} \\\\","\\midrule\\noalign{}","\\endhead","\\bottomrule\\noalign{}","\\endlastfoot","\\end{longtable}","","as","","\\begin{longtable}[]{@{}","  >{\\raggedright\\arraybackslash}p{(\\columnwidth - 2\\tabcolsep) * \\real{0.8947}}","  >{\\raggedright\\arraybackslash}p{(\\columnwidth - 2\\tabcolsep) * \\real{0.1053}}@{}}","\\toprule\\noalign{}","\\begin{minipage}[b]{\\linewidth}\\raggedright","\\[\\begin{array}{r}","a^{2}\\frac{\\partial^{2}\\zeta}{\\partial t^{2}} - D\\lbrack\\frac{\\partial}{\\partial\\lambda}(\\frac{\\partial}{\\partial\\lambda}(g\\zeta + U)) + \\frac{\\partial}{\\partial\\varphi}(\\frac{\\partial}{\\partial\\varphi}(g\\zeta + U))\\rbrack = 0","\\end{array}\\]","\\end{minipage} & \\begin{minipage}[b]{\\linewidth}\\raggedright","12-6","\\end{minipage} \\\\","\\midrule\\noalign{}","\\endhead","\\bottomrule\\noalign{}","\\endlastfoot","\\end{longtable}","","The \\emph{λ} terms are in longitude so that we can use a separation of","variables approach and create a spatial standing wave for ENSO,","\\emph{SW}(\\emph{s})~where \\emph{s} is a wavenumber.","","\\begin{longtable}[]{@{}","  >{\\raggedright\\arraybackslash}p{(\\columnwidth - 2\\tabcolsep) * \\real{0.8947}}","  >{\\raggedright\\arraybackslash}p{(\\columnwidth - 2\\tabcolsep) * \\real{0.1053}}@{}}","\\toprule\\noalign{}","\\begin{minipage}[b]{\\linewidth}\\raggedright","\\[\\begin{array}{r}","\\frac{\\partial^{2}\\zeta}{\\partial t^{2}} - D\\lbrack(SW(s)\\zeta) + \\frac{\\partial}{\\partial\\varphi}(\\frac{\\partial}{\\partial\\varphi}(g\\zeta + U))\\rbrack = 0","\\end{array}\\]","\\end{minipage} & \\begin{minipage}[b]{\\linewidth}\\raggedright","12-7","\\end{minipage} \\\\","\\midrule\\noalign{}","\\endhead","\\bottomrule\\noalign{}","\\endlastfoot","\\end{longtable}","","That is close to a variation of a Sturm-Liouville equation (see right),","which is a second-order linear differential equation featuring a","\\emph{weighting} or \\emph{density} function. The weighting function","makes the differential equation more difficult to solve, so that","different approaches are taken to find solutions than with linear","ordinary differential equations. One of the approaches applied is to","solve for the equation eigenfunctions via variational methods.","","To get to the Sturm-Liouville formulation for a plausible set of","boundary conditions, we make an adjoint connection between a change in","latitudinal forcing with a temporal change:","","\\begin{longtable}[]{@{}","  >{\\raggedright\\arraybackslash}p{(\\columnwidth - 2\\tabcolsep) * \\real{0.8947}}","  >{\\raggedright\\arraybackslash}p{(\\columnwidth - 2\\tabcolsep) * \\real{0.1053}}@{}}","\\toprule\\noalign{}","\\begin{minipage}[b]{\\linewidth}\\raggedright","\\[\\begin{array}{r}","\\frac{\\partial\\zeta}{\\partial\\varphi} = \\frac{\\partial\\zeta}{\\partial t}\\frac{\\partial t}{\\partial\\varphi}","\\end{array}\\]","\\end{minipage} & \\begin{minipage}[b]{\\linewidth}\\raggedright","12-11","\\end{minipage} \\\\","\\midrule\\noalign{}","\\endhead","\\bottomrule\\noalign{}","\\endlastfoot","\\end{longtable}","","This allows a time-varying ``wiggle'' in the equatorial latitude","\\(\\varphi = f(t),\\ \\) perhaps physically analogous to the north-south","cyclic deflections observed in tropical instability waves along the","equator {[}36{]}.","","After properly applying the chain rule, this reduces the equation to a","function of \\emph{ζ}(\\emph{t})~and \\emph{φ}(\\emph{t}), along with a","constant \\emph{A}. The \\emph{A} subsumes the wavenumber \\emph{SW(s)}","portion so there will be multiple solutions for the various standing","waves, which will be used in fitting the model to the data.","","\\begin{longtable}[]{@{}","  >{\\raggedright\\arraybackslash}p{(\\columnwidth - 2\\tabcolsep) * \\real{0.8947}}","  >{\\raggedright\\arraybackslash}p{(\\columnwidth - 2\\tabcolsep) * \\real{0.1053}}@{}}","\\toprule\\noalign{}","\\begin{minipage}[b]{\\linewidth}\\raggedright","\\[A\\zeta(t) + \\frac{1}{\\frac{\\partial\\varphi}{\\partial t}} \\cdot \\frac{\\partial}{\\partial t}\\frac{\\zeta^{'}(t)}{\\frac{\\partial\\varphi}{\\partial t}} = 0\\]","\\end{minipage} & \\begin{minipage}[b]{\\linewidth}\\raggedright","12-12","\\end{minipage} \\\\","\\midrule\\noalign{}","\\endhead","\\bottomrule\\noalign{}","\\endlastfoot","\\end{longtable}","","So if we fix \\emph{φ}(\\emph{t})~to a periodic function with a long-term","mean of zero","","\\begin{longtable}[]{@{}","  >{\\raggedright\\arraybackslash}p{(\\columnwidth - 2\\tabcolsep) * \\real{0.8947}}","  >{\\raggedright\\arraybackslash}p{(\\columnwidth - 2\\tabcolsep) * \\real{0.1053}}@{}}","\\toprule\\noalign{}","\\begin{minipage}[b]{\\linewidth}\\raggedright","\\[\\begin{array}{r}","\\frac{\\partial\\varphi}{\\partial t} = \\sum_{i = 1}^{i = N}{k_{i}\\omega_{i}\\cos(\\omega_{i}t)}","\\end{array}\\]","\\end{minipage} & \\begin{minipage}[b]{\\linewidth}\\raggedright","12-13","\\end{minipage} \\\\","\\midrule\\noalign{}","\\endhead","\\bottomrule\\noalign{}","\\endlastfoot","\\end{longtable}","","to describe the perturbed tractive latitudinal displacement terms near","the equator, then the solution is the following potentially highly","nonlinear result (depending on the strength of the inner terms):","","\\begin{longtable}[]{@{}","  >{\\raggedright\\arraybackslash}p{(\\columnwidth - 2\\tabcolsep) * \\real{0.8947}}","  >{\\raggedright\\arraybackslash}p{(\\columnwidth - 2\\tabcolsep) * \\real{0.1053}}@{}}","\\toprule\\noalign{}","\\begin{minipage}[b]{\\linewidth}\\raggedright","\\[\\begin{array}{r}","\\zeta(t) = \\sin(\\sqrt{A}\\sum_{i = 1}^{i = N}{k_{i}\\sin(\\omega_{i}t) + \\theta_{0})}","\\end{array}\\]","\\end{minipage} & \\begin{minipage}[b]{\\linewidth}\\raggedright","12-14","\\end{minipage} \\\\","\\midrule\\noalign{}","\\endhead","\\bottomrule\\noalign{}","\\endlastfoot","\\end{longtable}","","where \\emph{A}~is an aggregate of the constants of the differential","equation and \\emph{θ}0~represents the fixed phase offset necessary for","aligning on a seasonal peak. This approximation of a tangential tractive","force as a cyclic displacement for \\emph{φ}(\\emph{t})~is a subtle yet","very effective means of eliminating a big unknown in the dynamics ( this","is similar to a Berry phase applied as a cyclic adiabatic","process{[}37{]}, {[}38{]}, with the phase being the internal sin","modulation). The bottom-line of not including this approximation is that","it would leave us with an indeterminate set of equations; but by making","the substitution, we achieve a closed-form analytical result which is","conducive to model fitting.","","Now consider that the ENSO itself is precisely the","∂\\emph{v/}∂\\emph{t}~~term - the horizontal longitudinal acceleration of","the fluid, i.e. leading to the observed sloshing in the thermocline -","which can be derived from the above by applying the solution to","Laplace\\textquotesingle s third tidal equation in simplified form above:","","\\begin{longtable}[]{@{}","  >{\\raggedright\\arraybackslash}p{(\\columnwidth - 2\\tabcolsep) * \\real{0.8947}}","  >{\\raggedright\\arraybackslash}p{(\\columnwidth - 2\\tabcolsep) * \\real{0.1053}}@{}}","\\toprule\\noalign{}","\\begin{minipage}[b]{\\linewidth}\\raggedright","\\[\\frac{\\partial v}{\\partial t} = \\cos(\\sqrt{A}\\sum_{i = 1}^{i = N}{k_{i}\\sin(\\omega_{i}t)} + \\theta_{0})\\]","\\end{minipage} & \\begin{minipage}[b]{\\linewidth}\\raggedright","12-15","\\end{minipage} \\\\","\\midrule\\noalign{}","\\endhead","\\bottomrule\\noalign{}","\\endlastfoot","\\end{longtable}","","There is also a \\emph{cosh} solution for when \\emph{A} is negative","","\\begin{longtable}[]{@{}","  >{\\raggedright\\arraybackslash}p{(\\columnwidth - 2\\tabcolsep) * \\real{0.8947}}","  >{\\raggedright\\arraybackslash}p{(\\columnwidth - 2\\tabcolsep) * \\real{0.1053}}@{}}","\\toprule\\noalign{}","\\begin{minipage}[b]{\\linewidth}\\raggedright","\\[\\frac{\\partial v}{\\partial t} = \\cosh(\\sqrt{A}\\sum_{i = 1}^{i = N}{k_{i}\\sin(\\omega_{i}t)} + \\theta_{0})\\]","\\end{minipage} & \\begin{minipage}[b]{\\linewidth}\\raggedright","12-16","\\end{minipage} \\\\","\\midrule\\noalign{}","\\endhead","\\bottomrule\\noalign{}","\\endlastfoot","\\end{longtable}","","This result is simply a \\emph{F=ma} response to a tractive gravitational","forcing which obeys Laplace\\textquotesingle s tidal equations. The","\\emph{cos} solutions show a negative feedback while the \\emph{cosh}","solutions are the positive feedback variation.","","The multiple solutions are important as they provide a set of standing","mode waves that may match the modes observed in the Pacific ocean.","Starting with the main ENSO standing wave which essentially describes","the Darwin/Tahiti dipole (low wave number = smallest \\(\\sqrt{A})\\) , it","can also capture any faster and shorter spatial-scale standing Kelvin","waves that appear (high wave number = larger \\(\\sqrt{A}).\\) This is","shown in \\textbf{FIGURE\\_12-3 on page 317} where the standing-wave","sinusoids are superimposed on the equatorial sea-surface temperature","(SST) profile. This becomes a limited set of dispersive modes that","comprise ENSO, which is in contrast to the unlimited number of modes","that comprise ordinary wind waves that will be described in the section","on stochastic aquatic waves.","","\\hypertarget{part-2-deriving-the-lunar-forcing-periods}{%","\\paragraph*{Part 2 : Deriving the lunar forcing","periods}\\label{part-2-deriving-the-lunar-forcing-periods}}","\\addcontentsline{toc}{paragraph}{Part 2 : Deriving the lunar forcing","periods}","","\\includegraphics[width=2.54236in,height=1.01458in]{media/image1.png}It","is well known that a periodic forcing can reduce the erratic","fluctuations and uncertainty of a near-chaotic response function","{[}39{]}, {[}40{]}. One of the features of the ENSO time-series is a","strong biennial component, manifesting as an alternating sign seasonal","impulse. A biennial or 2-year modulation agrees with that found by","Dunkerton {[}41{]} and Remsberg {[}42{]} in stratospheric measurements","and by Pan {[}28{]} in sensitive GPS of the earth's deformation. A","strict biennial mode is also observed in ENSO measurements {[}43{]}.","","To model this characteristic, we apply a seasonal aliasing to the lunar","gravitational pull to generate the terms needed as an effective forcing","stimulus. This turns into a set of harmonics which we can fit the data","to.","","The starting premise is that a known lunar tidal forcing signal is","periodic","","\\begin{longtable}[]{@{}","  >{\\raggedright\\arraybackslash}p{(\\columnwidth - 2\\tabcolsep) * \\real{0.8947}}","  >{\\raggedright\\arraybackslash}p{(\\columnwidth - 2\\tabcolsep) * \\real{0.1053}}@{}}","\\toprule\\noalign{}","\\begin{minipage}[b]{\\linewidth}\\raggedright","\\[L(t) = k \\cdot sin(\\omega_{L}t + \\phi)\\]","\\end{minipage} & \\begin{minipage}[b]{\\linewidth}\\raggedright","12-17","\\end{minipage} \\\\","\\midrule\\noalign{}","\\endhead","\\bottomrule\\noalign{}","\\endlastfoot","\\end{longtable}","","The seasonal signal is likely a strong periodic delta function, which","peaks at a specific time of the year. This can be approximated as a","Fourier series of period 2\\emph{π}.","","\\begin{longtable}[]{@{}","  >{\\raggedright\\arraybackslash}p{(\\columnwidth - 2\\tabcolsep) * \\real{0.8947}}","  >{\\raggedright\\arraybackslash}p{(\\columnwidth - 2\\tabcolsep) * \\real{0.1053}}@{}}","\\toprule\\noalign{}","\\begin{minipage}[b]{\\linewidth}\\raggedright","\\[s(t) = \\sum_{i = 1}^{n}{a_{i}sin(2\\pi ti + \\theta_{i})}\\]","\\end{minipage} & \\begin{minipage}[b]{\\linewidth}\\raggedright","12-18","\\end{minipage} \\\\","\\midrule\\noalign{}","\\endhead","\\bottomrule\\noalign{}","\\endlastfoot","\\end{longtable}","","For now, the exact form of this doesn\\textquotesingle t matter, as what","we are trying to show is how the aliasing comes about.","","The forcing is then a combination of the lunar cycles \\emph{L}(\\emph{t})","amplified in some way by the strongly cyclically peaked seasonal signal","\\emph{s}(\\emph{t}).","","\\begin{longtable}[]{@{}","  >{\\raggedright\\arraybackslash}p{(\\columnwidth - 2\\tabcolsep) * \\real{0.8947}}","  >{\\raggedright\\arraybackslash}p{(\\columnwidth - 2\\tabcolsep) * \\real{0.1053}}@{}}","\\toprule\\noalign{}","\\begin{minipage}[b]{\\linewidth}\\raggedright","\\[f(t) = s(t)L(t)\\]","\\end{minipage} & \\begin{minipage}[b]{\\linewidth}\\raggedright","12-19","\\end{minipage} \\\\","\\midrule\\noalign{}","\\endhead","\\bottomrule\\noalign{}","\\endlastfoot","\\end{longtable}","","Multiplying this out, and pulling the lunar factor into the sum","","\\begin{longtable}[]{@{}","  >{\\raggedright\\arraybackslash}p{(\\columnwidth - 2\\tabcolsep) * \\real{0.8947}}","  >{\\raggedright\\arraybackslash}p{(\\columnwidth - 2\\tabcolsep) * \\real{0.1053}}@{}}","\\toprule\\noalign{}","\\begin{minipage}[b]{\\linewidth}\\raggedright","\\[f(t) = k\\sum_{i = 1}^{n}{a_{i}sin(\\omega_{L}t + \\phi)sin(2\\pi ti + \\theta_{i})}\\]","\\end{minipage} & \\begin{minipage}[b]{\\linewidth}\\raggedright","12-20","\\end{minipage} \\\\","\\midrule\\noalign{}","\\endhead","\\bottomrule\\noalign{}","\\endlastfoot","\\end{longtable}","","then with the trig identity","","\\begin{longtable}[]{@{}","  >{\\raggedright\\arraybackslash}p{(\\columnwidth - 2\\tabcolsep) * \\real{0.8947}}","  >{\\raggedright\\arraybackslash}p{(\\columnwidth - 2\\tabcolsep) * \\real{0.1053}}@{}}","\\toprule\\noalign{}","\\begin{minipage}[b]{\\linewidth}\\raggedright","\\[sin(x)sin(y) = \\frac{1}{2}(cos(x - y) - cos(x + y))\\]","\\end{minipage} & \\begin{minipage}[b]{\\linewidth}\\raggedright","12-21","\\end{minipage} \\\\","\\midrule\\noalign{}","\\endhead","\\bottomrule\\noalign{}","\\endlastfoot","\\end{longtable}","","Expanding the lower frequency difference terms and ignoring the higher","frequency additive terms","","\\begin{longtable}[]{@{}","  >{\\raggedright\\arraybackslash}p{(\\columnwidth - 2\\tabcolsep) * \\real{0.8947}}","  >{\\raggedright\\arraybackslash}p{(\\columnwidth - 2\\tabcolsep) * \\real{0.1053}}@{}}","\\toprule\\noalign{}","\\begin{minipage}[b]{\\linewidth}\\raggedright","\\[f(t) = k/2\\sum_{i = 1}^{n}{a_{i}\\sin\\left( \\left( \\omega_{L} - 2\\pi i \\right)t + \\psi_{i} \\right)\\  + \\ ...}\\]","\\end{minipage} & \\begin{minipage}[b]{\\linewidth}\\raggedright","12-22","\\end{minipage} \\\\","\\midrule\\noalign{}","\\endhead","\\bottomrule\\noalign{}","\\endlastfoot","\\end{longtable}","","Thus we can now understand how the high frequency lunar tidal","\\emph{ω\\textsubscript{L}}~~terms get reduced in frequency by multiples","of 2\\emph{π}, until it nears the period of the seasonal cycle. These are","the Fourier harmonics of the aliased lunar cycles that comprise the","forcing. The aliasing explains why we do not see the ENSO cycles at the","monthly scale, but instead observed cycles at the multi-year scale.","","In a more precise fashion, we can apply the known gravitational forcing","from the lunar orbit and the interaction with the sun\\textquotesingle s","yearly (lunar-perturbed) cycle. This works very effectively, and the","closer one can obtain the precise orbital path, the better resolved the","fit. The same physical aliasing phenomena occurs with the atmospheric","QBO described in the previous chapter, where it is readily apparent with","an applied annual impulse which modulates the draconic or nodal lunar","forcing.","","An interesting mathematical consideration is how best to adopt the","biennial modulation. Either of these mechanisms work equivalently to","some degree: (1) alternate the sign of the biennial forcing impulse, (2)","apply a 2-year Mathieu modulation to the DiffEq (see text","box)\\footnote{According to Frandsen {[}24{]}, any vertical forcing to","  the RHS of the sloshing differential equation gives rise to a","  Mathieu-type modulation of the same frequency.}, or (3) apply a 1","year-delay as a delayed differential equation. These are equivalent in","the sense that similar results can be obtained if only one of these are","used or they are all used, but each scaled by 1/3. For the Laplace","formulation, approach (1) provides the most convenient approach because","it does not require a rework to the closed-form solution.","","\\hypertarget{application}{%","\\paragraph*{Application}\\label{application}}","\\addcontentsline{toc}{paragraph}{Application}","","The \\textbf{Part 1} derivation provides the closed-form natural response","and \\textbf{Part 2} provides the boundary condition forcing terms due to","the lunar nodal cycle. As an example of a typical fit with this","approach, we apply the gravitational forcing as:","","\\begin{longtable}[]{@{}","  >{\\raggedright\\arraybackslash}p{(\\columnwidth - 2\\tabcolsep) * \\real{0.8947}}","  >{\\raggedright\\arraybackslash}p{(\\columnwidth - 2\\tabcolsep) * \\real{0.1053}}@{}}","\\toprule\\noalign{}","\\begin{minipage}[b]{\\linewidth}\\raggedright","\\[F(t) \\propto - \\frac{a^{'}(t) + d^{'}(t)}{(R_{0} + a(t) + d(t))^{3}}\\]","\\end{minipage} & \\begin{minipage}[b]{\\linewidth}\\raggedright","12-24","\\end{minipage} \\\\","\\midrule\\noalign{}","\\endhead","\\bottomrule\\noalign{}","\\endlastfoot","\\end{longtable}","","where \\emph{a(t)} and \\emph{d(t)} are the composed monthly and","fortnightly anomalistic and draconic lunar cycles. The \\emph{a(t)} term","is the anomalistic lunar distance and \\emph{d(t)} is the draconic or","nodal perturbation to the distance. Note the prime indicating that the","forcing applied is the derivative of the conventional inverse squared","Newtonian attraction. This generates an inverse cubic formulation","corresponding to the consensus analysis describing a differential tidal","force.","","This nonlinear forcing generates a rich set of harmonics that expand as","a Fourier series used as input to the Laplace solution, especially when","the \\emph{a(t)} and \\emph{d(t)} terms approach \\emph{R\\textsubscript{0}}","in value. We can either expand these as harmonics (as is done in","conventional tidal analysis) or retain the inverse law form above, which","provides a stronger constraint. There is a rationale for this","constraint. When the inverse cube expression is expanded, the","Taylor\\textquotesingle s series of the forcing potential becomes a","power-law polynomial corresponding to the lunar harmonic terms. The","chief characteristic of the polynomial is the alternating sign for each","successive power, which has implications for convergence under certain","regimes. What happens with the alternating sign is that each of the","added harmonics will highly compensate the previous underlying","harmonics, giving the impression that pulling one signal out will","scramble the fit. This is conceptually no different than eliminating any","one term from a sine or cosine Taylor\\textquotesingle s series, which","are also all compensating with alternating sign. Because we need to keep","those terms for high precision modeling, we also need to be wary of","possible over-fitting of these terms --- as the solver does not realize","that the values for those terms have the constraint that they derive","from the original Taylor\\textquotesingle s series. It\\textquotesingle s","not really a problem for conventional tidal analysis, as the signals are","so clean, but for the noisy ENSO time-series, this is an issue.","","In practice the solution to this predicament is not to do the Taylor","series harmonic fitting at all, but leave it in the form of the inverse","power law. That makes a lot of sense --- and the only reason for not","doing this until now is probably due to the inertia of conventional","wisdom, in that it wasn\\textquotesingle t necessary for tidal analysis","where the harmonics decomposition performs adequately. So the more","fundamental formulation is what we will use, as leaving the forcing as","an inverse power-law has clear benefits.","","\\textbf{FIGURE\\_12-4 on page 317} shows the model fit to an ENSO","Southern Oscillation Index (SOI) time-series {[}44{]}, where the","training interval is set to 1900-1920. The SOI time series was chosen","because it is well characterized {[}44{]}and functions close to the","oscillating standing-wave dipole {[}45{]} that is characteristic of a","sloshing behavior\\footnote{Although the SOI is a measure of atmospheric","  pressure, via the reverse barometric effect one can tie in ocean-level","  variations as a result of spatio-temporal sloshing to changes in","  atmospheric pressure.}. It also has a long-running record dating back","130+ years collected from the Tahiti (+ pole) and Darwin (- pole) sites.","The cross-validation outside this interval is relative good in spite of","the almost over-fitting used for the training.","","\\textbf{FIGURE\\_12-5 on page 317} is the model fit over the entire","interval, which relaxes the over-fitting in the shorter interval, but","keeps the lunar tidal amplitude and phase factors largely intact.","","An excursion-limiting behavior is imposed by a \\emph{sin}(\\(\\sqrt{A}\\)","\\emph{sin}(\\emph{t}))~modulation, and this formulation can also show","\\emph{amplitude folding} at the positive and negative extremes. In other","words, if the amplitude is too large, the outer \\emph{sin}~modulation","starts to \\emph{shrink} the excursion, instead of just limiting it. When","the amplitude starts folding and bifurcating in this fashion (as","\\emph{A} grows larger), it falls into the behavioral category identified","\\emph{as wave breaking}. As a likely application of this breaking, we","can substitute a larger \\emph{A} factor in Laplace\\textquotesingle s","solution to model the more rapid cycles observed in the ENSO SOI","time-series; these would arise from a differing wavenumber (likely","larger index) standing wave solution to the sloshing (see FIG.12-3).","This enables a fit over a wide dynamic range of time-scales without","adding many additional degrees of freedom, i.e. only an altered \\emph{A}","term and phase.","","As with QBO, by carefully applying these factors within the tidal","formulation, we can generate a concise solution that has all the","characteristics of the measured ENSO. The main challenge in this work is","to perform meticulous bookkeeping of the orbital cycles, keeping track","of all the second-order perturbations to the lunar cycles. This can also","be validated against sensitive LOD measurements of the","earth\\textquotesingle s slight angular rotation variations (see Chapter","13). \\textbf{FIGURE\\_12-6 on page 317} (a) shows the variation needed to","describe the second-order variation in the anomalistic cycle while (b)","is for the draconic cycle.","","This reference {[}46{]}, {[}47{]} provides an algorithm for the","anomalistic Earth-Moon distance perturbation, where an accurate mapping","to the valley excursions has the strongest gravitational impact and thus","is most sensitive to a good fit. This is sampled at 1 month intervals,","which reveals some aliasing as the anomalistic month is shorter than a","calendar month. The valley excursions show the greatest sensitivity in","the model and therefore that needs to track most closely to","Meeus\\textquotesingle{} algorithm. Second-order perturbation in the","Draconic cycle frequency is described at the NASA moon orbit","site\\footnote{NASA moon orbit reference web site :","  https://eclipse.gsfc.nasa.gov/SEhelp/moonorbit.html}. Both these","modifications are vital to achieve a good model fit to ENSO.","","After fitting in the time domain, \\textbf{FIGURE\\_12-7 on page 317}","shows the comparison of the power spectra, which has a higher","correlation coefficient than in the time domain. No dominant spectral","peaks occur, which is why many researchers categorize ENSO as a","stochastic red-noise process.","","To get an example of the noise, we can compare a sliding correlation","coefficient of the model against SOI and then compare SOI against","another ENSO index, that of the NINO34 temperature index as shown in","\\textbf{FIGURE\\_12-8 on page 317}. Note that the lower correlations","between model and SOI match the regions showing lower correlations","between NINO34 and SOI, indicating that these are likely regions of poor","signal-to-noise ratio.","","In terms of sensitivity to the chosen lunar periods, if we adjust those","from the nominal values, the fit rapidly degrades as shown in","\\textbf{FIGURE\\_12-9 on page 317} with values shown in Table 12-2.","","Table 12-2","","\\begin{longtable}[]{@{}","  >{\\raggedright\\arraybackslash}p{(\\columnwidth - 16\\tabcolsep) * \\real{0.0599}}","  >{\\raggedright\\arraybackslash}p{(\\columnwidth - 16\\tabcolsep) * \\real{0.1285}}","  >{\\raggedright\\arraybackslash}p{(\\columnwidth - 16\\tabcolsep) * \\real{0.1033}}","  >{\\raggedright\\arraybackslash}p{(\\columnwidth - 16\\tabcolsep) * \\real{0.1024}}","  >{\\raggedright\\arraybackslash}p{(\\columnwidth - 16\\tabcolsep) * \\real{0.1396}}","  >{\\raggedright\\arraybackslash}p{(\\columnwidth - 16\\tabcolsep) * \\real{0.1396}}","  >{\\raggedright\\arraybackslash}p{(\\columnwidth - 16\\tabcolsep) * \\real{0.1210}}","  >{\\raggedright\\arraybackslash}p{(\\columnwidth - 16\\tabcolsep) * \\real{0.1024}}","  >{\\raggedright\\arraybackslash}p{(\\columnwidth - 16\\tabcolsep) * \\real{0.1033}}@{}}","\\toprule\\noalign{}","\\begin{minipage}[b]{\\linewidth}\\raggedright","~","\\end{minipage} & \\begin{minipage}[b]{\\linewidth}\\raggedright","~","\\end{minipage} & \\multicolumn{7}{l@{}}{%","\\begin{minipage}[b]{\\linewidth}\\raggedright","adjusted draconic","\\end{minipage}} \\\\","\\midrule\\noalign{}","\\endhead","\\bottomrule\\noalign{}","\\endlastfoot","~ & ~ & 27.2 & 27.205 & 27.21 & 27.21222 & 27.215 & 27.22 & 27.3 \\\\","\\multirow{7}{*}{adjusted anomalistic} & 27.5 & 0.4208 & 0.3536 & 0.5856","& 0.55481 & 0.4866 & 0.3252 & 0.3059 \\\\","& 27.545 & 0.3584 & 0.2578 & 0.3864 & 0.578559 & 0.5438 & 0.3837 &","0.3769 \\\\","& 27.55 & 0.4487 & 0.5308 & 0.5486 & 0.5312 & 0.5192 & 0.5114 &","0.4273 \\\\","& 27.55455 & 0.5151 & 0.5297 & 0.643268 & \\textbf{0.6604} & 0.62944 &","0.5203 & 0.4974 \\\\","& 27.555 & 0.5262 & 0.5028 & 0.632 & 0.65486 & 0.6207 & 0.4916 &","0.4275 \\\\","& 27.56 & 0.4333 & 0.4362 & 0.4675 & 0.56948 & 0.515 & 0.5338 &","0.5002 \\\\","& 27.6 & 0.3973 & 0.4321 & 0.5468 & 0.519975 & 0.4546 & 0.5201 &","0.4501 \\\\","\\end{longtable}","","As further evidence to the scaling effect of tidal periods to annual","periods, consider \\textbf{FIGURE\\_12-10 on page 317.} Here, if the","nominal values for the lunar draconic, anomalistic, and tropical monthly","cycles are adjusted slightly, the value for the calendar year needed to","be adjusted linearly. This is in keeping with the physical aliasing","effect that an annual (or biennial) cycle would have after modulating a","monthly cycle.","","The ENSO model based on Laplace\\textquotesingle s tidal equation needs","to be carefully considered as a physically-sound alternative to the","chaotic network model published by Tsonis in a highly influential","physics journal {[}13{]}. As is the case of Lindzen\\textquotesingle s","model of the QBO, a generation of climate science may have followed","Tsonis\\textquotesingle{} lead down a rabbit hole of unnecessary","complexity in understanding ENSO.","","\\textbf{Summary}","","For a combination of monthly and fortnightly sinusoidal terms for","\\emph{a(t)} and \\emph{d(t)} (suitably modified for nonlinear nodal and","perigean corrections due to the synodic/tropical cycle) the search","routine rapidly converges to an optimal ENSO fit.~ It does this more","quickly than the harmonic analysis, which requires at least double the","unknowns for the additional higher-order factors needed to capture the","tidally forced response waveform. One of the keys is to collect the","chain rule terms \\emph{a\\textquotesingle(t)} and","\\emph{d\\textquotesingle(t)} in the numerator; without these, the","necessary mixed terms which multiply the anomalistic and draconic","signals do not emerge strongly.","","As described, a strictly biennial modulation needs to be applied to this","forcing to capture the measured ENSO dynamics~--- this is a","period-doubling pattern observed in hydrodynamic systems with a strong","fundamental (in this case annual) and is climatologically explained by a","persistent year-to-year regenerative feedback in the SLP and SST","anomalies. There exists the known tendency to locking of El Nino events","towards the end of a calendar year {[}23{]} which does not contradict an","underlying biennial modulation as \\emph{``its phase is strongly locked","to the calendar months. Thus, the assumption of the 2-year periodicity","seems reasonable''} {[}43{]}. The relative impact of El Nino events tend","to be associated with the biennial periodicity.","","Apart from the difficulty of predicting significant regime changes that","can violate the stationarity requirement of the model, both hindcasting","for evaluating paleoclimate ENSO data {[}48{]} and forecasting may have","potential applications for this model. The model is simple enough in its","formulation that others can readily improve in its fidelity without","resorting to the complexity of a full blown global circulation model","(GCM), and in contrast to those depending on erratic {[}49{]} or","stochastic inputs which have less predictive power. Fits to a red noise","model over a short training interval as shown in FIG.12-4 will quickly","diverge over the entire range since the coherence of the long term","periodic forcing factors do not apply. So this ENSO model has a real","potential for producing deterministic predictions years in advance.","","\\hypertarget{stochastic-aquatic-waves}{%","\\subsubsection*{Stochastic Aquatic","Waves}\\label{stochastic-aquatic-waves}}","\\addcontentsline{toc}{subsubsection}{Stochastic Aquatic Waves}","","Ocean waves exist in as disordered and unpredictable state as the wind.","We may not always notice this as the scale of waves is smaller and often","takes the form of a regular lapping of swells. In practice, the wind and","wave energy distributions relate via similar maximum entropy disorder","considerations. The following derivation assumes a deep enough water","such that the wave troughs do not touch bottom.","","In the wild, waves usually display little coherence over long spatial","scales. In other words, the knowledge that one has about one wave has","limited implications regarding another wave separated by several","undulations. Gravity plays a critical role as it automatically detrends","the data to maintain a level profile over the distances of interest. Sea","waves also are very sensitive to long-range coherence, that is, the","ability to maintain phase relationships over many wavelengths such as","seen with capillary waves{[}50{]}{[}51{]} or over a significant distance","as with cnoidal swells{[}52{]}. The term coherence is defined as","correlation when applied specifically to wave-like properties {[}53{]}.","This is noteworthy also when one considers that a dispersion relation","holds between the spatial frequencies and temporal frequencies of","aquatic waves.","","\\hypertarget{spatially-incoherent-wave-spectra}{%","\\paragraph*{Spatially Incoherent Wave","Spectra}\\label{spatially-incoherent-wave-spectra}}","\\addcontentsline{toc}{paragraph}{Spatially Incoherent Wave Spectra}","","First, we make a maximum entropy estimation of the energy of a","one-dimensional propagating wave driven by a prevailing wind direction.","The mean energy of the wave is related to the wave height by the square","of the height, \\emph{\\textbf{H}}. This makes sense because a taller wave","needs a broader base to support that height, leading to a scaled","pseudo-triangular shape of a \\emph{gravity} wave, as shown in","\\textbf{FIGURE\\_12-11 on page 318}.","","Since the area of such a scaled triangle goes as","\\emph{H}\\textsuperscript{2}, the MaxEnt cumulative probability is:","","\\begin{longtable}[]{@{}","  >{\\raggedright\\arraybackslash}p{(\\columnwidth - 2\\tabcolsep) * \\real{0.8947}}","  >{\\raggedright\\arraybackslash}p{(\\columnwidth - 2\\tabcolsep) * \\real{0.1053}}@{}}","\\toprule\\noalign{}","\\begin{minipage}[b]{\\linewidth}\\raggedright","\\[P(H) = \\ e^{- aH^{2}}\\]","\\end{minipage} & \\begin{minipage}[b]{\\linewidth}\\raggedright","12-25","\\end{minipage} \\\\","\\midrule\\noalign{}","\\endhead","\\bottomrule\\noalign{}","\\endlastfoot","\\end{longtable}","","where \\emph{a} is related to the mean energy of an ensemble of waves.","This relationship is empirically observed from measurements of ocean","wave heights over a sufficient time period. However, we can proceed","further and try to derive the dispersion results of wave frequency,","which is the typical oceanography measure. So we consider --- based on","the energy stored in a specific wave --- the time, \\emph{t}, it will","take to drop a height, \\emph{H}, by the Newton\\textquotesingle s law","relation:","","\\begin{longtable}[]{@{}","  >{\\raggedright\\arraybackslash}p{(\\columnwidth - 2\\tabcolsep) * \\real{0.8947}}","  >{\\raggedright\\arraybackslash}p{(\\columnwidth - 2\\tabcolsep) * \\real{0.1053}}@{}}","\\toprule\\noalign{}","\\begin{minipage}[b]{\\linewidth}\\raggedright","\\[t^{2}\\ \\sim\\ H\\]","\\end{minipage} & \\begin{minipage}[b]{\\linewidth}\\raggedright","12-26","\\end{minipage} \\\\","\\midrule\\noalign{}","\\endhead","\\bottomrule\\noalign{}","\\endlastfoot","\\end{longtable}","","and since \\emph{t} goes as 1/\\emph{f}, then we can create a new PDF from","the height cumulative as follows:","","\\begin{longtable}[]{@{}","  >{\\raggedright\\arraybackslash}p{(\\columnwidth - 2\\tabcolsep) * \\real{0.8947}}","  >{\\raggedright\\arraybackslash}p{(\\columnwidth - 2\\tabcolsep) * \\real{0.1053}}@{}}","\\toprule\\noalign{}","\\begin{minipage}[b]{\\linewidth}\\raggedright","\\[p(f)df = \\frac{dP(H)}{dH}\\frac{dH}{df}df\\]","\\end{minipage} & \\begin{minipage}[b]{\\linewidth}\\raggedright","12-27","\\end{minipage} \\\\","\\midrule\\noalign{}","\\endhead","\\bottomrule\\noalign{}","\\endlastfoot","\\end{longtable}","","where {[}54{]}","","\\begin{longtable}[]{@{}","  >{\\raggedright\\arraybackslash}p{(\\columnwidth - 2\\tabcolsep) * \\real{0.8947}}","  >{\\raggedright\\arraybackslash}p{(\\columnwidth - 2\\tabcolsep) * \\real{0.1053}}@{}}","\\toprule\\noalign{}","\\begin{minipage}[b]{\\linewidth}\\raggedright","\\[H\\ \\sim\\ \\frac{1}{f^{2}}\\]","","\\[\\frac{dH}{df}\\ \\sim - \\frac{1}{f^{3}}\\]","\\end{minipage} & \\begin{minipage}[b]{\\linewidth}\\raggedright","12-28","\\end{minipage} \\\\","\\midrule\\noalign{}","\\endhead","\\bottomrule\\noalign{}","\\endlastfoot","\\end{longtable}","","Then","","\\begin{longtable}[]{@{}","  >{\\raggedright\\arraybackslash}p{(\\columnwidth - 2\\tabcolsep) * \\real{0.8947}}","  >{\\raggedright\\arraybackslash}p{(\\columnwidth - 2\\tabcolsep) * \\real{0.1053}}@{}}","\\toprule\\noalign{}","\\begin{minipage}[b]{\\linewidth}\\raggedright","\\[p(f)\\ \\sim\\frac{1}{f^{5}}e^{- c/f^{4}}\\]","\\end{minipage} & \\begin{minipage}[b]{\\linewidth}\\raggedright","12-29","\\end{minipage} \\\\","\\midrule\\noalign{}","\\endhead","\\bottomrule\\noalign{}","\\endlastfoot","\\end{longtable}","","which is the Pierson-Moskowitz wave spectra that oceanographers have","observed for years (developed first in 1964, variations of this include","the JONSWAP, Bretschneider and ITTC wave spectra{[}55{]} derived from","the more general Kolmogorov energy spectrum {[}56{]}). A more detailed","analysis of the wave equation leads to the concept of \\emph{frequency","dispersion}, where the spatial wavelength and temporal period are","related for a wide range of spatial and temporal combinations.","\\footnote{This is in contrast to the limited frequency dispersion","  observed in ENSO, where only certain standing wave modes are allowed,","  with a one-to-one mapping of temporal frequency to spatial frequency,","  described in the previous section.}\\\\","\\strut \\\\","This concise derivation works well despite not applying the correct path","of calculating an auto-correlation from","\\emph{p}\\textbf{(}\\emph{f}\\textbf{)} and then deriving a power spectrum","from the Fourier Transform of \\emph{p\\textbf{(}f\\textbf{)}.} As smooth","ocean waves approach a sinusoidal ideal, a single wave will contribute a","Fourier component at that frequency alone and the spectrum can be","evaluated almost by inspection. This convenient shortcut remains useful","in understanding the simple physics and probabilities involved.\\\\","\\strut \\\\","The utility of this derived form can be demonstrated using data{[}57{]}","obtained from public-access stations. The following data is extracted","from a pair of measuring stations located off the coast of San Diego","{[}58{]}, see \\textbf{FIGURE\\_12-12 on page 318}.","","The data shown here is averaged wave spectra for the entire day","1/1/2012. The open circles in \\textbf{FIGURE\\_12-13 on page 318}","correspond to best fits from the derived MaxEnt algorithm applied to the","measured data set. \\footnote{The dataset is available from :\\\\","  http://cdip.ucsd.edu/?nav=historic\\&sub=data\\&units=metric\\&tz=UTC\\&pub=public\\&map\\_stati=1,2,3\\&stn=167\\&stream=p1\\&xyrmo=201201\\&xitem=product25}","","Like many similar spectra such as wind and EMI, the wave spectrum","derives simply from maximum entropy conditions.","","Note that we did not need to invoke the full spectral decomposition","model presented earlier. The correlations are short over the sinusoidal","shape of an individual wave and so those frequency components show up","strongly in the energy density PSD. The implications are that the","driving forcing function needed to provide order in the case of natural","waves is missing, and this makes sense as the main stimulus, wind","energy, on its own is highly disordered. The wind simply stimulates the","wave to maximize its entropy subject to the kinetic and potential energy","that are provided.","","On the other hand, under controlled or reinforcing (positive","interference) conditions, more order can be supplied to make the waves","appear more coherent over a spatial distance.","","\\hypertarget{spatially-coherent-wave-spectra}{%","\\paragraph*{Spatially Coherent Wave","Spectra}\\label{spatially-coherent-wave-spectra}}","\\addcontentsline{toc}{paragraph}{Spatially Coherent Wave Spectra}","","Coherent waves are easier to generate in the laboratory than in nature,","apart from the occasional cnoidal swells peculiar to a region. A large","wave tank, with waves generated by a consistent wind will clearly expose","any ordered features in the PSD {[}59{]}.","","In \\textbf{FIGURE\\_12-14 on page 318} below, taken from {[}60{]}, the","measured PSD clearly shows clear harmonic peaks strongly suggestive of","longer-range order in the wave periodicities. Superimposed on the data","profile (shown in black) is a model adapted from the derivation of the","spectra of a semi-Markovian distribution of steps in Chapter 16. Note","that from the spatial/temporal dispersion relation, the time- domain","frequency scale with the spatial-domain frequencies, as short, choppy","waves have much smaller periods or cycle-times than the large, rolling","waves. This means that we can apply a spatial analysis to infer the","temporal characteristics with some generality (and vice versa).","","The basic model is to choose a \\emph{P}(\\emph{L}) wave density such that","it reproduces the spacing and envelope of the harmonics. One of the","simplest density functions is known as the shifted or delayed","exponential. This maintains a minimum spacing with the possibility of an","occasional longer wavelength:","","\\begin{longtable}[]{@{}","  >{\\raggedright\\arraybackslash}p{(\\columnwidth - 2\\tabcolsep) * \\real{0.8947}}","  >{\\raggedright\\arraybackslash}p{(\\columnwidth - 2\\tabcolsep) * \\real{0.1053}}@{}}","\\toprule\\noalign{}","\\begin{minipage}[b]{\\linewidth}\\raggedright","\\[P(L) = \\left\\{ \\begin{aligned}","0,\\ \\  & L \x3C L_{0} \\\\","e^{- \\alpha(L - L_{0})},\\ \\  & L > L_{0}","\\end{aligned} \\right.\\ \\]","\\end{minipage} & \\begin{minipage}[b]{\\linewidth}\\raggedright","12-30","\\end{minipage} \\\\","\\midrule\\noalign{}","\\endhead","\\bottomrule\\noalign{}","\\endlastfoot","\\end{longtable}","","This generates a Fourier transform","","\\begin{longtable}[]{@{}","  >{\\raggedright\\arraybackslash}p{(\\columnwidth - 2\\tabcolsep) * \\real{0.8947}}","  >{\\raggedright\\arraybackslash}p{(\\columnwidth - 2\\tabcolsep) * \\real{0.1053}}@{}}","\\toprule\\noalign{}","\\begin{minipage}[b]{\\linewidth}\\raggedright","\\[P\\left( S_{x} \\right) = \\ \\frac{e^{- iS_{x}L_{0}}}{1 + \\frac{iS_{x}}{\\alpha}}\\]","\\end{minipage} & \\begin{minipage}[b]{\\linewidth}\\raggedright","12-31","\\end{minipage} \\\\","\\midrule\\noalign{}","\\endhead","\\bottomrule\\noalign{}","\\endlastfoot","\\end{longtable}","","And then the full power spectrum assuming a coherent relationship","exists:","","\\begin{longtable}[]{@{}","  >{\\raggedright\\arraybackslash}p{(\\columnwidth - 2\\tabcolsep) * \\real{0.8947}}","  >{\\raggedright\\arraybackslash}p{(\\columnwidth - 2\\tabcolsep) * \\real{0.1053}}@{}}","\\toprule\\noalign{}","\\begin{minipage}[b]{\\linewidth}\\raggedright","\\[I\\left( S_{x} \\right) = \\ \\frac{1}{\\left( S_{x} - \\alpha\\sin\\left( S_{x}L_{0} \\right) \\right)^{2} + \\ {\\alpha^{2}(1 + \\cos\\left( S_{x}L_{0} \\right))}^{2}}\\]","","\\[I\\left( S_{x} \\right) = \\ \\frac{1}{\\left( S_{x} + \\alpha\\sin\\left( {2S}_{x}L_{0} \\right) \\right)^{2} + \\ {\\alpha^{2}(1 - \\cos\\left( {2S}_{x}L_{0} \\right))}^{2}} - \\ \\frac{1}{{S_{x}}^{2}{(1 + \\alpha L_{0})}^{2}}\\]","\\end{minipage} & \\begin{minipage}[b]{\\linewidth}\\raggedright","12-32","\\end{minipage} \\\\","\\midrule\\noalign{}","\\endhead","\\bottomrule\\noalign{}","\\endlastfoot","\\end{longtable}","","These two formulations, scaled accordingly, generate the semi-Markov","harmonic dispersion model shown in \\textbf{FIG.12-14}. The first","equation generates an odd-harmonic waveform due to a symmetric view of","up and down steps. The second equation assumes an all-harmonic","composition derived by assuming a surface with a saw-tooth set of steps,","which is likely nearer the real situation for stimulated waves.","","Note that as \\emph{L\\textsubscript{0}} approaches zero, then the Markov","random walk spectrum is recovered. Thus a simple parametric model with","the two coefficients related by a \\emph{characteristic period,}","\\textless{} \\emph{L} \\textgreater, can emulate the continuum of order to","disorder:","","\\begin{longtable}[]{@{}","  >{\\raggedright\\arraybackslash}p{(\\columnwidth - 2\\tabcolsep) * \\real{0.8947}}","  >{\\raggedright\\arraybackslash}p{(\\columnwidth - 2\\tabcolsep) * \\real{0.1053}}@{}}","\\toprule\\noalign{}","\\begin{minipage}[b]{\\linewidth}\\raggedright","\\[\\left\\langle L \\right\\rangle = L_{0} + 1/\\alpha\\]","\\end{minipage} & \\begin{minipage}[b]{\\linewidth}\\raggedright","12-33","\\end{minipage} \\\\","\\midrule\\noalign{}","\\endhead","\\bottomrule\\noalign{}","\\endlastfoot","\\end{longtable}","","As \\emph{L\\textsubscript{0}} approaches \\textless{} \\emph{L}","\\textgreater{} the spectrum will show strong harmonics. As","\\emph{L\\textsubscript{0}} approaches 0, the random aspect will suppress","the harmonics. As an example of this continuum consider","\\textbf{FIGURE\\_12-15 on page 318} which shows very weak harmonics and","higher disorder than the waves of \\textbf{FIG.12-14} {[}61{]}","","Note that the dispersion model does, by definition, generate square or","sharp edges. Unless there are breakers in the waves, the actual profiles","will become rounded. By applying a low-pass filter to the model, the","actual waveform will reveal reduced high frequency components and give","better agreement with the spectral data. Another example, fit from a","simulated wave {[}62{]} is shown in \\textbf{FIGURE\\_12-16 on page 318}.","","In general, aquatic wave coherence showing strong harmonics occurs under","more controlled conditions than normally exist in the wild.","","\\hypertarget{wave-height-distributions}{%","\\paragraph*{Wave Height Distributions}\\label{wave-height-distributions}}","\\addcontentsline{toc}{paragraph}{Wave Height Distributions}","","Since the area of such a scaled triangle goes as \\emph{H}","\\textsuperscript{2}, the MaxEnt cumulative probability is:","","\\begin{longtable}[]{@{}","  >{\\raggedright\\arraybackslash}p{(\\columnwidth - 2\\tabcolsep) * \\real{0.8947}}","  >{\\raggedright\\arraybackslash}p{(\\columnwidth - 2\\tabcolsep) * \\real{0.1053}}@{}}","\\toprule\\noalign{}","\\begin{minipage}[b]{\\linewidth}\\raggedright","\\[P(H) = e^{- \\alpha H^{2}}\\]","\\end{minipage} & \\begin{minipage}[b]{\\linewidth}\\raggedright","12-34","\\end{minipage} \\\\","\\midrule\\noalign{}","\\endhead","\\bottomrule\\noalign{}","\\endlastfoot","\\end{longtable}","","where \\emph{α} is related to the mean energy of an ensemble of waves.","This relationship is empirically observed from measurements of ocean","wave heights over a sufficient time period. This looks at height alone,","with variations in height given by \\textbf{FIGURE\\_12-17 on page 318}","given by a statistical wave distribution in \\textbf{FIGURE\\_12-18 on","page 318}.","","Long-lived ocean and lake measuring stations have recorded historical","records of wave crest data over the span of decades. From the US Army","Corps of Engineer's Wave Information Studies~project{[}63{]}, the","following figure collects chop and swell data from over several hundred","million data points on Lake Michigan, see \\textbf{FIGURE\\_12-19 on page","318}.","","The probability density function for significant wave height empirically","follows a modified Bessel function of the second kind. This essentially","derives from a maximum entropy distribution for a wave height with a","mean energy value, which is then dispersed again by maximizing the","uncertainty in the energy. With precisely the same pattern that we","derive the Bessel function from a range in wind speed values, we can","derive the PDF for wave energy.","","The Bessel fit works well for small wave heights but then starts to","diverge when the wave height starts to exceed a critical level. This","critical level is approximately the cresting limit of a wave given the","average depth of the water. At this point we can apply an empirical","correction factor first proposed by Jahns and Wheeler{[}64{]} and","further analyzed by Haring {[}65{]}. This factor is a","2\\textsuperscript{nd}-order polynomial which gradually suppresses the","wave height from exceeding the critical cresting value.","","\\begin{longtable}[]{@{}","  >{\\raggedright\\arraybackslash}p{(\\columnwidth - 2\\tabcolsep) * \\real{0.8947}}","  >{\\raggedright\\arraybackslash}p{(\\columnwidth - 2\\tabcolsep) * \\real{0.1053}}@{}}","\\toprule\\noalign{}","\\begin{minipage}[b]{\\linewidth}\\raggedright","\\[1 - 4.37\\frac{H}{d}(0.57 - \\frac{H}{d})\\]","\\end{minipage} & \\begin{minipage}[b]{\\linewidth}\\raggedright","12-35","\\end{minipage} \\\\","\\midrule\\noalign{}","\\endhead","\\bottomrule\\noalign{}","\\endlastfoot","\\end{longtable}","","The rationale for the factor arises from the remote likelihood of a wave","height from exceeding the average water depth (\\emph{d}) in a region.","","Based on data collected from coastal waters of two large lakes, Superior","and Michigan, and that along the eastern USA seaboard of the Atlantic","Ocean, we can see the characteristic bend on the PDF at approximately 10","meter height.\\footnote{The data from Lake Superior was not as extensive","  as the other two bodies of water}","","The same basic Jahns/Wheeler correction is applied across the bodies of","water. Both Michigan and Superior use a Bessel function of order 1,","while the Atlantic uses a Bessel of order 2, which is generated by","assuming an uncertainty that is not a maximum entropy exponential in the","mean, but a MaxEnt that is a gamma of order 2 (i.e. two exponentials","convolved which reduces the variance in ½), see \\textbf{FIGURE\\_12-20 on","page 318}.","","Table 12-3: Heuristic Jahns/Wheeler correction used different","coefficients from the Fixed Haring to achieve the best fit.","","\\begin{longtable}[]{@{}","  >{\\raggedright\\arraybackslash}p{(\\columnwidth - 8\\tabcolsep) * \\real{0.2537}}","  >{\\raggedright\\arraybackslash}p{(\\columnwidth - 8\\tabcolsep) * \\real{0.1973}}","  >{\\raggedright\\arraybackslash}p{(\\columnwidth - 8\\tabcolsep) * \\real{0.2030}}","  >{\\raggedright\\arraybackslash}p{(\\columnwidth - 8\\tabcolsep) * \\real{0.1730}}","  >{\\raggedright\\arraybackslash}p{(\\columnwidth - 8\\tabcolsep) * \\real{0.1729}}@{}}","\\toprule\\noalign{}","\\begin{minipage}[b]{\\linewidth}\\raggedright","\\end{minipage} & \\begin{minipage}[b]{\\linewidth}\\raggedright","Atlantic Seaboard","\\end{minipage} & \\begin{minipage}[b]{\\linewidth}\\raggedright","Lake Superior","\\end{minipage} & \\begin{minipage}[b]{\\linewidth}\\raggedright","Lake Michigan","\\end{minipage} & \\begin{minipage}[b]{\\linewidth}\\raggedright","Fixed Haring","\\end{minipage} \\\\","\\midrule\\noalign{}","\\endhead","\\bottomrule\\noalign{}","\\endlastfoot","Inferred Average Depth & 25.13 m & 22.82 m & 13.75 m & - \\\\","Haring Coefficient 1 & 2.52 & 4.86 & 3.03 & 4.37 \\\\","Haring Coefficient 2 & 1.03 & 0.69 & 0.90 & 0.57 \\\\","\\end{longtable}","","To evaluate the fit across the extreme values, \\textbf{FIGURE\\_12-21 on","page 318} shows the model profile on a log-log scale. The long tails are","important for evaluating the probabilities of high sea-state values.","","Lake Superior measurements (\\textbf{FIGURE\\_12-22 on page 318}) were","sparse, yet the same profile is observed.","","Wave measurements from the Atlantic Coast along the length of the USA","were less widely dispersed (see \\textbf{FIGURE\\_12-23 on page 318}). It","is much more unlikely to find very calm waters in the data set. In this","case, a higher order BesselK function was used to model the wave height","distribution.","","In general, the divergence between the BesselK maximum entropy estimator","for wind and that for waves is due to nonlinear effects at large","wave-height. The heuristic Jahns/Wheeler correction factor is physically","modeled by wave cusping, which generate larger heights than the","triangular base wave predicts. This essentially rationalizes the","sharpened crests and flattened troughs before it hits the cresting","limit. We used a heuristic, but other corrections are available, such as","derivations from the Rayleigh-Stokes (Tayfun model{[}66{]} {[}67{]})","process which is classified as a narrow-banded random process{[}68{]}.","","For use in simulations, other factors also play in such as, the","probability of consecutive waves and the fact that phase velocity","increases with the increase in wave steepness{[}54{]} .","","Even with the complexity inherent in modeling turbulence, the modeling","at the PDF level has some predictive power. For example, using the","Atlantic model parameters we can estimate the wave height for the","Mediterranean coast of Greece {[}69{]}. In this sense, waves have","universal characteristics. But as with the more deterministic","conventional tidal analysis, the data first needs to be calibrated and","then assumptions of stationarity need to be maintained.","","\\hypertarget{potential-for-prediction}{%","\\subsubsection*{Potential for","Prediction}\\label{potential-for-prediction}}","\\addcontentsline{toc}{subsubsection}{Potential for Prediction}","","Of the two classes of waves, deterministic and stochastic, deterministic","behaviors have the most potential for both short and long-term","prediction. As the ENSO behavior shows strong evidence of determinism,","analogous to that of ocean tides, there should be hope for creating a","robust prediction model. The requirements for the classical prediction","of ocean tides are that (1) we have a clean low-noise signal and (2)","that high resolution data comprising an interval nearing the length of","the combined repeat cycle is available.","","To estimate the repeat cycle of the ENSO model described earlier in this","chapter, we need to determine the combination of periods that generate a","base reinforcement interval. First consider that the repeat cycle must","align on a multiple of a year. The three lunar cycles thus also align on","a year. This means that interactions of the nodal, anomalistic, and","tropical months must also align on whole multiples of a year. The","closest repeat period for the combined 18.6 yr, 6 yr, and 8.85 yr","pairwise repeat cycles is 372 years {[}70{]} (and this is still","approximate with respect to the anomalistic-tropical beat cycle). For","very short intervals there is a likelihood for over-fitting.
